L31: ## I. Executive Summary
L32: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L33: On September 30, 2022, Novo Nordisk, Inc. submitted an original New Drug Application for Rivfloza® (nedosiran) for the treatment of primary hyperoxaluria type 1 (PH1) in adult and pediatric patients (b) (4) years of age and older. Nedosiran is a subcutaneously administered, synthetic, double-stranded small interfering ribonucleic acid that inhibits the messenger RNA of the lactate dehydrogenase A gene, thus reducing urinary oxalate levels.
L34: In support of the proposed indication, the Applicant submitted the results of an adequate and well-controlled trial that enrolled 35 patients with PH1 or primary hyperoxaluria type 2 (PH2) and an estimated glomerular filtration rate \ge 30 mL/min/1.73 m^2 (Study DCR-PHXC-201). The Applicant also submitted data from an ongoing single-arm extension study of Study DCR-PHXC-201 in which all patients were treated with nedosiran (Study DCR-PHXC-301), as confirmatory evidence of effectiveness.
L35: Although the development program, as originally designed, was intended to support an indication for the treatment of both PH1 and PH2, (b) (4)
L36: the Applicant proposed marketing approval only for patients with PH1.
L37: The review team has concluded that the application provides substantial evidence of nedosiran's effectiveness in reducing urinary oxalate levels, a surrogate for important clinical manifestations of PH1, including nephrolithiasis, its related complications, and loss of kidney function in patients with relatively preserved kidney function (generally an estimated glomerular filtration rate &gt; 30 mL/min/1.73 m^2). The safety database is limited because of the size of the affected population but does not raise significant concerns and is acceptable in the context of this rare disease. As such, the review team believes the data provide compelling evidence of a benefit that outweighs the risks and recommends approval, and the signatory authority agrees. Given that the study did not enroll participants less than 9 years of age, the indication will be limited to patients 9 years of age and older with PH1.
L38: Although the Agency did not object to the Applicant's proposal to assess the efficacy of nedosiran using a primary efficacy endpoint of area under the concentration-time curve for percentage change from Baseline in 24 h urinary oxalate from study Days 90 to 180, such an endpoint may not be easily understood by prescribers of nedosiran. Therefore, information will also be included in labeling on the least-squares mean percentage change from Baseline in 24 h urinary oxalate excretion (corrected for body surface area in patients &lt;18 years of age) averaged over Days 90, 120, 150, and 180.
L39: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L40: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L41: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L42: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Primary hyperoxaluria type 1 (PH1) is a rare, serious disease that often manifests in childhood or adolescence.
PH1 is caused by an inherited deficiency of the liver enzyme alanine-glyoxylate aminotransferase (AGT), which leads to overproduction of oxalate by the liver.
Patients with PH1 have recurrent kidney stones and can have deposition of calcium in the kidneys (nephrocalcinosis), leading to a loss of kidney function. As kidney function declines, oxalate accumulates in the body and deposits in other tissues, leading to systemic manifestations, including arrhythmias and cardiac arrest, gangrene, bone/joint pain and fractures, blindness, peripheral neuropathy, dental abnormalities, skin and subcutaneous nodules, plaques, ulceration, pancytopenia, and hypothyroidism.
PH1 is a rare, serious genetic disease that usually presents in childhood with recurrent kidney stones and complications thereof including infection, pain, and urinary tract obstruction. Manifestations of more advanced disease include kidney failure, bone and joint abnormalities, cardiac arrhythmias and arrest, blindness, peripheral neuropathy, dental abnormalities, skin and subcutaneous nodules, plaques, ulceration, pancytopenia, and hypothyroidism.
L43: ## NDA 215842
## Rivfloza (nedosiran)
L44: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current treatment options	Lumasiran is approved to lower urinary and plasma oxalate levels in pediatric and adult patients with PH1. Urinary oxalate is a surrogate for kidney stones and loss of kidney function in patients with PH1 with relatively preserved kidney function (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] >30 mL/min/1.73 m2 or serum creatinine within normal for patients <12 months of age) and plasma oxalate is a surrogate for the systemic manifestations of PH1 in patients with an eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m2 including patients on hemodialysis.
Pyridoxine may reduce hepatic oxalate production in patients with certain genetic mutations.
Patients are treated with high fluid intake and medications such as citrate and magnesium to reduce calcium oxalate crystallization, though many progress despite these interventions.
Liver transplant replaces the missing AGT enzyme and is essentially curative but is associated with substantial morbidity.
There is an unmet need for additional therapies to treat PH1. Nedosiran targets a different enzyme than the currently approved lumasiran.
L45: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	To establish benefit, the Applicant primarily relied upon the results of an adequate and well-controlled trial (Study DCR-PHXC-201) that enrolled 35 patients with PH1 or PH2 and an eGFR \ge 30 mL/min/1.73 m2.(b) (4) the Applicant proposed marketing approval only for patients with PH1.
Study DCR-PHXC-201 met its primary endpoint (the area under the concentration-time curve for percentage change from Baseline in 24 h urinary oxalate from study Days 90 to 180) in the overall trial population, with a statistically persuasive p-value (p<0.0001).
Among the patients with PH1, the mean change from baseline in 24 h urinary oxalate levels averaged over Days 90, 120, 150, and 180 was -50% with nedosiran compared to 6% with placebo (treatment difference -56%; 95% confidence interval [CI] -80%, -33%), reflecting a substantial reduction in urinary oxalate levels with nedosiran.
In an exploratory analysis in the overall population, eGFR decreased at a rate of 1.1 mL/min/1.73 m2/month in the placebo group and 0.35 mL/min/1.73 m2/month in the nedosiran group for a between-group difference of -0.75 mL/min/1.73 m2/month (95% CI -2.1, 0.6). The percentage change in the number of measurable kidney stones on ultrasound in the overall population from baseline to Day 180 was assessed as an exploratory endpoint and showed no difference between the two arms.
Nedosiran substantially reduces 24 h urinary oxalate levels in participants with PH1. Given the pathophysiology of PH1 and the available epidemiologic data, the effects on urinary oxalate are expected to translate to benefits on kidney stone events and kidney function. Given that calcium oxalate stones are slow to form and pass, and the rate of loss of kidney function in the population, such effects were not established nor were they expected over the relatively short duration of followup. Nedosiran will add another treatment option for patients \ge 9 years of age with PH1 and relatively preserved kidney function.
L46: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and risk management	After 6 months of treatment in Study DCR-PHXC-201, patients could enroll in an ongoing single-arm extension study, DCR-PHXC-301, in which all patients were treated with nedosiran. Among the eight patients randomized to placebo in DCR-PHXC-201 who received nedosiran for 6 months in DCR-PHXC-301, 24 h urinary oxalate decreased from a mean of 1872 \mumol/day (95% CI: 851, 2894) at the end of DCR-PHXC-201 to 816 \mumol/day (95% CI: 591, 1042), reflecting a mean percent decrease of 46% (95% CI: 24%, 67%). The reduction in urinary oxalate was maintained in the 13 patients with PH1 who received nedosiran in DCR-PHXC-201 and received an additional 6 months of treatment in Study DCR-PHXC-301.
Nedosiran was generally well-tolerated; however, the safety database was small and of limited duration.
The most common adverse events were injection site reactions, which occurred throughout the study period, were generally mild, and did not lead to treatment discontinuation.
The safety database is acceptable in the context of this rare disease. Nedosiran was well-tolerated. Risks were generally mild and included injection site reactions. There were no apparent significant or irreversible toxicities.
L47: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L48: Data from DCR-PHXC-201 demonstrate that nedosiran leads to a meaningful decrease in 24 h urinary oxalate levels in participants with PH1 and relatively preserved kidney function. Given this size of the treatment effect and knowledge of the pathophysiology of the disease, these findings are expected to translate to a benefit on important clinical manifestations of the disease, including nephrolithiasis, related complications, and loss of kidney function. Approval is supported for patients down to 9 years of age based on the population enrolled in the study. The most common risk was injection site reactions, which were generally mild, and did not lead to treatment discontinuation. There were no other apparent significant or irreversible toxicities. As such, the benefits outweigh the risks.
L49: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L50: ## 3. Introduction
L51: ## Disease Background
L52: Primary hyperoxaluria type 1 (PH1) is the most common and severe type of primary hyperoxaluria, a group of rare genetic inborn errors of hepatic glyoxylate metabolism characterized by overproduction of oxalate by the liver. To date, three types of PH have been described, defined by the affected gene. PH1 accounts for 70 to 80% of PH cases and is caused by recessively inherited mutations in the alanine-glyoxylate aminotransferase gene, which lead to a deficiency of the hepatic peroxisomal enzyme alanine glyoxylate aminotransferase (AGT). Under normal circumstances, AGT catalyzes the conversion of glyoxylate to glycine. AGT deficiency results in accumulation of glyoxylate, which is rapidly metabolized to oxalate, a poorly soluble organic acid that is excreted by the kidneys.
L53: In patients with PH1, oxalate binds to calcium, saturates the urine with calcium oxalate, and deposits in various parts of the kidneys, leading to the primary clinical manifestations of the disease. Patients present, usually in childhood, with symptoms and sequelae of calcium oxalate crystalluria, recurrent urolithiasis, urinary tract infections, nephrocalcinosis, kidney scarring, and eventual kidney failure. As kidney function declines, urinary excretion of oxalate decreases and oxalate accumulates in the body, leading to systemic oxalosis, a debilitating condition that can cause arrhythmias, cardiac arrest, gangrene, bone and joint pain, reduced mobility, fractures, blindness, peripheral neuropathy, dental abnormalities, skin and subcutaneous nodules, plaques, ulceration, pancytopenia, and hypothyroidism. When moderate to severe kidney failure develops, patients require combined or sequential liver and kidney transplant, often preceded by a period on intensive dialysis.
L54: To date, one therapy has been approved for the treatment of PH1. On November 23, 2020, FDA approved lumasiran to lower urinary oxalate levels, a surrogate for kidney stones and loss of kidney function, in pediatric and adult patients with PH1 who have relatively preserved kidney function. Lumasiran is a small interfering ribonucleic acid (siRNA) that reduces levels of the glycolate oxidase enzyme by targeting the hydroxyacid oxidase 1 (HAO1) messenger ribonucleic acid (mRNA) in hepatocytes. On October 6, 2022, lumasiran was also approved to lower plasma oxalate, a surrogate for systemic manifestations of PH1, in patients with more advanced kidney disease.
L55: Other general measures that are used in patients with PH1 include increased fluid intake to maintain a brisk flow of dilute urine as well as the administration of agents such as citrate and magnesium supplements to increase urinary oxalate solubility. Pyridoxine, a coenzyme of AGT, is variably effective in some genotypes that result in mislocalization of AGT within the hepatocyte. Liver transplant is curative but is associated with significant morbidity.
L56: ## Nedosiran
L57: Nedosiran is a subcutaneously administered, synthetic, double-stranded siRNA conjugated to an N-acetyl-D-galactosamine (GalNAc) ligand. The ligand is designed to target delivery to hepatocytes via asialoglycoprotein receptors (ASPGRs). The siRNA inhibits the mRNA of the LDHA gene, which encodes LDH, the enzyme that metabolizes glyoxylate to oxalate. Suppression of LDH should, therefore, reduce hepatic oxalate production, in turn reducing oxaluria and its sequelae.
L58: ## Urinary Oxalate as a Surrogate for Clinical Outcomes in PH1
L59: Although the manifestations of PH1 can be serious, calcium oxalate stones are slow to form and pass, and loss of kidney function is generally gradual. Given the rarity of the disease, it would be challenging to detect effects on clinical events over the course of a feasible development program. As such, the nedosiran development program was designed to assess effects on urinary oxalate excretion. Support for the use of urinary oxalate as a surrogate for clinical outcomes in PH1 was based primarily on:
L60: - Knowledge of the pathophysiology of the disease and the causal role of urinary oxalate in kidney stone formation, nephrocalcinosis, and loss of kidney function;
- Epidemiologic data showing an association between urinary oxalate and loss of kidney function, particularly in patients with high levels of urinary oxalate; and
- Observational data from patients treated with pyridoxine or liver transplant showing associations between reductions in urinary oxalate and preservation of kidney function.
L61: During discussions of the design of the trial, the Agency expressed a willingness to accept a substantial change in urinary oxalate in patients with high baseline levels as a basis for full approval. As previously noted, another siRNA, lumasiran, was approved in 2020 to lower urinary oxalate levels in pediatric and adult patients with PH1 with relatively preserved kidney function based on such an effect.

L79: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L80: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L81: ## 5.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
L82: Nedosiran is a dicer-substrate siRNA oligonucleotide conjugated with GalNAc amino sugar residues for preferential uptake by hepatocytes via the asialoglycoprotein receptor (ASGPR). The antisense strand for nedosiran is designed to target LDHA mRNA via the RNA interference (RNAi) pathway, thereby reducing the levels of LDHA mRNA and LDH protein, leading to decreased production of oxalate by the liver.
L83: The pharmacological characteristics of nedosiran were studied in cynomolgus monkeys and mice to assess on-target and off-target hybridization effects, respectively. The target sequence of nedosiran in LDHA mRNA is conserved in humans and nonhuman primates, but not in mice; thus, nedosiran is fully active in cynomolgus monkeys but inactive in mice. The effects of knockdown of LDHA mRNA were also assessed in genetically or chemically induced mouse models of PH using the pharmacologically active mouse-specific analog DCR-m355.
L84: ## 5.1.2. Proof of Concept for Efficacy in Animal Models
L85: The nonclinical pharmacology data support proof of concept, as well as the dosing regimen of nedosiran that was evaluated in the clinical studies based on the following data:
L86: - Administration of nedosiran to mice expressing human LDHA mRNA by an adeno-associated virus system resulted in a significant reduction of LDHA mRNA. A single subcutaneous (SC) dose of 1 to 6 mg/kg resulted in reduction of LDHA mRNA by approximately 90% for a 4-week duration, with recovery towards baseline by Week 6 postdose. The long duration of action of nedosiran indicates sustained silencing of LDHA mRNA and provides a rationale for the clinical dosing frequency of once every 4 weeks.
- A single SC dose of nedosiran 1 or 3 mg/kg to cynomolgus monkeys reduced LDHA mRNA expression by 29% and 72%, respectively, indicating that a single low dose of 1 mg/kg is a potentially effective dose.
- No age-related differences in the level of expression of ASGPR mRNA were observed in human liver from 0 to 2-year-olds versus adults, indicating that the relative uptake of the GalNAc-conjugated siRNA in the liver is unlikely to be affected by age.
L87: - In a mouse model of PH1 with knockout of the alanine-glyoxylate aminotransferase gene involved in limiting glyoxylate oxidation to oxalate, administration of the mouse active analog of nedosiran (DCR-m355) at doses of 0.5, 1, and 5 mg/kg caused dose-dependent suppression of hepatic LDH protein, which correlated with a reduction in the urinary oxalate-to-creatinine ratio, indicating that inhibition of hepatic LDHA mRNA expression reduced oxalate production.
- Administration of DCR-m355 (5 mg/kg) to mice subjected to running on a treadmill resulted in reduced expression of LDHA mRNA in the liver, with no effect on skeletal muscle LDHA mRNA expression or changes in measures of skeletal muscle LDHA isozyme activity as assessed using circulating lactate, pyruvate and creatine kinase levels, thus demonstrating selective knockdown of liver LDHA mRNA.
L88: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology / Pharmacokinetics
L89: **Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L90: Characteristic	Drug Information
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	Pharmacologic Activity
Nedosiran is a lactate dehydrogenase A (LDHA)-directed small interfering ribonucleic acid (siRNA) indicated for the treatment of primary hyperoxaluria type 1 (PH1) to lower urinary oxalate levels in pediatric and adult patients.
Mechanism of action	Nedosiran is a double-stranded siRNA that reduces production of oxalate by the liver by targeting LDHA messenger ribonucleic acid (mRNA) via the RNA interference pathway.
Active moieties	Nedosiran
QT prolongation	Safety pharmacology studies in monkeys did not demonstrate QT or QTc (Bazett's; QTcB) prolongation after three subcutaneous repeat doses of 30 and 300 mg/kg administered on Days 1, 4, and 10, respectively. The IC50 for the inhibitory effect of nedosiran on human ether-a-go-go (hERG) potassium current is estimated to provide a safety margin of >390-fold compared to the unbound maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) values in patients receiving the maximum recommended dose (160 mg once monthly) of nedosiran. Exposures achieved after clinical doses of nedosiran are not expected to produce meaningful hERG-related QT prolongation.
General Information

The performance of bioanalytical methods and the results of quantification were acceptable.

L91: Characteristic	Drug Information
Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	Doses that were evaluated were either body weight based (3.3 mg/kg in 9 to 11-year-olds) or weight band-based in adolescents and adults (160 mg for \ge 50 kg and 128 mg for <50 kg) and targeted near-complete inhibition of oxalate production in the liver. With the above dosing, 24 h urinary oxalate was reduced by a placebo-adjusted least-squares mean average (standard error [SE]) of 59% (11%) from Baseline in PH1 participants in Study DCR-PHXC-201
Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	A maximum tolerated dose (MTD) was not determined. The highest tested clinical dose was 12 mg/kg (single ascending dose in healthy participants, Study DCR-PHXC-101)
Dose proportionality	Nedosiran exhibited a dose-proportional increase in plasma exposure following single subcutaneous doses from 1.5 to 6.0 mg/kg (Study DCR-PHXC-103)
Accumulation	No accumulation in plasma is observed with once-monthly dosing
Time to achieve steady state	Maximal reduction in mean urinary oxalate from baseline was achieved between 3 to 4 months with once monthly dosing
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial/study formulations	The to-be-marketed formulations – a vial (0.5 mL) and a prefilled syringe (0.8 mL or 1 mL) – were used for subcutaneous administration in the clinical trials

Distribution

L92: Characteristic	Drug Information
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	The dosing is once-monthly (Q1M) based on weight bands and body weight. For adults and adolescents (\ge 12 years) weighing \ge 50 kg and <50 kg, the Q1M dose is 160 mg and 128 mg, respectively. (b) (4)
Age	The youngest patient enrolled in the trials was \sim 9 years old. Based on population PK/pharmacodynamics (PD) modeling, age was not a significant covariate that influenced the PK or the PD response to nedosiran in the age range of 9 years and older. Dose adjustment is not needed based on age in this age range.
Renal impairment	No dosage adjustment is needed for patients with mild or moderate impairment of renal function. The efficacy of nedosiran is not established in patients with severe renal impairment.
Hepatic impairment	No dosage adjustment is needed for patients with mild impairment of hepatic function. Nedosiran was not studied in patients with moderate or severe impairment of hepatic function.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	Nedosiran did not show any direct or time-dependent inhibition of any of the major CYP enzymes in vitro at clinically relevant concentrations. Nedosiran did not induce CYP1A2, 2B6 or 3A4 at clinically relevant concentrations.
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	Nedosiran was not an inhibitor of uptake or efflux drug transporters.
Immunogenicity
Bioanalysis	Antidrug antibodies (ADA) to nedosiran were detected using a validated enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay. This method used an aliquot of 30 \mu\text{L} of human serum with an assay minimum required dilution of 1:10.
Incidence	There was no treatment-emergent ADA among the 59 patients tested with the ADA assay. Three patients tested positive for ADA at screening and one of them continued to have ADA titers, which continually decreased during the 12-month treatment period.
Clinical impact	There were no differences observed in the PK, PD response, and safety of nedosiran in patients that tested-positive for ADA at screening.

L94: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L95: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L96: The Applicant proposed the dosing regimen for adults and pediatric patients (b) years of age and older shown in Table 6.
L97: **Table 6. Applicant-Proposed Dosing Regimen for Patients (b) Years of Age and Older**
L98: Age	Body Weight	Dosing Regimen
Adults and adolescents

L99: Source: Proposed-uspi, Table 1.
L100: The dose level in mg refers to nedosiran free base weight.
L101: In the randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind PHYOX2 study in pediatric and adult patients with PH1 and PH2 9 to 46 years of age, 23 participants received the proposed dosing regimen, which, as discussed elsewhere in this review, demonstrated an acceptable efficacy and safety profile.
L102: A population pharmacokinetics (PK) model was developed based on a dataset containing plasma nedosiran concentrations from Studies DCR-PHXC-101, DCR-PHXC-102, DCR-PHXC-103, DCR-PHXC-201, and DCR-PHXC-301, representing a total of 143 participants 9 to 73 years of age, weighing 31.8 kg to 132 kg. Given that maturation of kidney and hepatic function is close to completion at 6 years of age, and that a maturation function for estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) was included in the population PK model simulation, the nedosiran PK profile estimated by the model down to 6 years of age is considered acceptable.
L103: The population PK model simulation showed the three groups (patients \ge 12 years of age with body weight \ge 50 kg, patients \ge 12 years of age with body weight &lt; 50 kg, and pediatric patients aged 6 to &lt; 12 years of age) achieved a similar nedosiran area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) at steady state with the proposed dosing regimen. The steady-state maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) appeared to be 76% higher in pediatric patients 6 to &lt; 12 years of age (estimated median C_{max} of 896 ng/mL) compared with that in patients \ge 12 years of age with body weight \ge 50 kg (estimated median C_{max} of 510 ng/mL). Of note, in the Phase 1 Study DCR-PHXC-101, no concerning findings were seen in three healthy volunteers who received a single nedosiran dose of 12 mg/kg, which resulted in an arithmetic mean C_{max} over 4010 ng/mL (over two-fold higher than the maximum estimated steady state C_{max} [approximately 1500 ng/mL] in pediatric patients 6 to &lt; 12 years of age with the proposed dosing regimen). As discussed elsewhere in this review, data from clinical studies conducted in patients with PH have also not identified any major safety concerns for nedosiran. The higher pediatric C_{max} is within a safe range identified in nonclinical studies. In addition, a new dose cap of 128 mg proposed by the review team for pediatric patients 6 to &lt; 12 years of age could attenuate the increased C_{max} for pediatric patients. Hence, the higher estimated C_{max} at steady state for pediatric patients 6 to 12 years of age is not considered a major safety concern.
L104: To further support dose selection, a population PK/PD model was developed based on data from 46 participants with PH1 in Studies DCR-PHXC-101, DCR-PHXC-201, and DCR-PHXC-301. The 46 participants were between 9 to 46 years of age (only 1 pediatric participant [a 9-year-old] was &lt; 12 years of age), with body weight ranging from 35 kg to 116 kg. The covariate analysis did not reveal any statistically significant covariate effects. Because no patient &lt; 9 years of age was included in the PK/PD model development, the effect of age on the PK/PD relationship in pediatric patients below 9 years of age remains uncertain. PK/PD model simulations indicate that the proposed dosing regimen results in a similar 24 h Uox response in participants \ge 12 years of age with body weight \ge 50 kg, participants \ge 12 years of age with body weight &lt; 50 kg, and pediatric participants aged 9 to &lt; 12 years of age. See Section 14.5 for details.
L105: ## **Conclusions on the Proposed Dosing Regimen**
L106: Because the PK/pharmacodynamics (PD) model development did not include data from pediatric patients below 9 years of age, the effect of age on the PK/PD relationship is unknown for the age group 6 to &lt; 9 years of age.
L107: The review team also recommends including an additional dose cap at 128 mg for pediatric patients 9 to &lt; 12 years of age and body weight &lt; 50 kg to prevent a potential decrease in dose when 11-year-old patients who weigh &lt; 50 kg turn 12 years of age. The PKPD simulations also confirmed that the dose cap of 128 mg would not compromise efficacy in patients 9 to &lt; 12 years of age weighing &lt; 50 kg (see Section 14.6).
L108: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended to Demonstrate Efficacy
L109: ## 6.2.1. Study DCR-PHXC-201
L110: ## 6.2.1.1. Design, DCR-PHXC-201
L111: Trial DCR-PHXC-201 (Study 201), summarized in Table 3 was a 6-month, parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter trial that randomized 35 participants with primary hyperoxaluria 1 (PH1) or 2 (PH2) in a 2:1 ratio to nedosiran or placebo. Randomization was adaptive, with randomization ratios determined during enrollment stratified by age (6 to 11, 12 to 17, or \ge 18 years of age) and baseline eGFR (&lt;45 or \ge 45 mL/min/1.73 m^2). The primary endpoint was the area under the curve for the percentage change from Baseline in 24 h urinary oxalate (Uox) from Days 90 to 180 (Days 90 to 180), normalized by body surface area (BSA) if patients were less than 18 years of age. The overall schema for the study is provided in Figure 1.
L112: Figure 1. Schema for Study 201
L113: * An additional 7 days of screening may be added for participants who require repeated 24-hour urine collections or for repeat of initially unanalyzable screening laboratory test samples.
L114: Abbreviations: EOS = end of study; PK = pharmacokinetics; Uox = urinary oxalate
L115: Source: Figure 1 of the Clinical Study Report.
L116: ## 6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, DCR-PHXC-201
L117: ## Inclusion Criteria
L118: For inclusion in Study 201, patients were required to fulfill all of the following criteria:
L119: - At least 6 years of age, at the time of signing the informed consent/assent.
- Documented diagnosis of PH1 or PH2, confirmed by genotyping (historically available genotype information was acceptable for study eligibility).
- Twenty-four-hour Uox excretion \ge 0.7 mmol (adjusted per 1.73 \text{ m}^2 BSA in participants &lt;18 years of age) in both collections performed in the screening period. At least 12 participants must have had at least one 24 h Uox excretion \ge 1.6 mmol (adjusted per 1.73 \text{ m}^2 BSA in participants aged &lt;18 years).
- Less than 20% variation between the two 24 h urinary creatinine excretion values obtained during screening. Individuals who did not achieve &lt;20% variation between the two screening values may have undergone a second round of urine collection. An extra seven calendar days could have been added to the screening window for participants to complete a second urine collection. If potential participants again failed to achieve the within-20% variation, they were excluded from participation.
- eGFR at Screening \ge 30 \text{ mL/min}/1.73 \text{ m}^2.
- Male participants:A male participant with a female partner of childbearing potential must have agreed to use contraception during the treatment period and for at least 12 weeks after the last dose of study intervention and refrain from donating sperm during this period.
- Female participants:Not pregnant, not breastfeeding, and at least one of the following conditions applied:Not of childbearing potential
OR

- Of childbearing potential who agreed to follow the contraceptive guidance of the protocol during the treatment period and for at least 12 weeks after the last dose of study intervention
L120: ## Exclusion Criteria
L121: Patients were excluded if they met any of the following criteria:
L122: - Had a prior renal or hepatic transplantation, or planned transplantation within the study period.
- Was receiving dialysis, or anticipated requirement for dialysis during the study period.
- Plasma oxalate (Pox) &gt; 30 \mu \text{mol/L}.
- Documented evidence of clinical manifestations of systemic oxalosis (including preexisting retinal, heart, or skin calcifications, or history of severe bone pain, pathological fractures, or bone deformations).
L123: - Presence of any condition or comorbidities that would have interfered with study compliance or data interpretation or potentially impacted patient safety including, but not restricted to:Severe intercurrent illness
- Known causes of active liver disease/injury or transaminase elevation (e.g., alcoholic liver disease, nonalcoholic fatty liver disease/steatohepatitis)
- Physician concerns about intake of drugs of abuse or excessive alcohol intake, or history of excessive alcohol intake in the 2 years prior to enrollment
- History of serious mental illness that included, but was not limited to, schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, or severe depression requiring hospitalization or pharmacological intervention
- Clinically relevant history or presence of cardiovascular, respiratory, gastrointestinal, hematological, lymphatic, neurological, musculoskeletal, genitourinary, immunological diseases, including dermatological (e.g., rash, severe eczema or dermatitis), or connective tissue diseases or disorders.
- Use of an RNAi drug within the last 6 months.
- History of one or more of the following reactions to an oligonucleotide-based therapy:Severe thrombocytopenia (platelet count \le 100,000/\mu\text{L})
- Hepatotoxicity, defined as alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase (AST) &gt;3\times the upper limit of normal (ULN) and total bilirubin &gt;2\times ULN or international normalized ratio &gt;1.5
- Severe flu-like symptoms leading to discontinuation of therapy
- Localized skin reaction from the injection (graded severe) leading to discontinuation of therapy
- Coagulopathy/clinically significant prolongation of clotting time.
- Participants receiving pyridoxine, an unapproved standard of care for patients with PH1, must have been at a stable dose for at least 4 weeks prior to Day 1 and must have been willing to remain on the same stable dose throughout the study.
- Participation in any clinical study in which they received an investigational medical product within 4 months before Screening. For investigational medical products with the potential to reduce urine and/or Pox concentrations, these concentrations must have returned to historical baseline levels prior to Screening and Diagnostic assessments.
- Liver test abnormalities: ALT and/or AST &gt;1.5\times ULN for age and gender.
- Positive screening for hepatitis B surface antigen, hepatitis C virus antibodies, or human immunodeficiency virus-1 or -2 antibodies. If a participant had been tested in the past 3 months, medical record documentation of this testing could have been used for screening. Viral serology testing was not required in participants &lt;18 years of age.
- Positive urine drug screen (to include at minimum: amphetamines, barbiturates, cocaine, opiates, and benzodiazepines). Investigator discretion was allowed. Urine drug screening was not required in participants \le 12 years of age.
- Known hypersensitivity to nedosiran, or any of its ingredients.
- Inability or unwillingness to comply with the specified study procedures, including collection of 24 h urine samples.
L124: ## 6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, DCR-PHXC-201
L125: The primary endpoint and associated analyses in the final statistical analysis plan, dated July 16, 2021, were consistent with those proposed earlier in the protocol, finalized on August 13, 2020.
L126: See “Interpretability of Results for PH2” for further discussion.
L127: ## **Primary Endpoint**
L128: The primary endpoint, 24 h Uox AUC of percentage change from Baseline calculated from Uox data collected on Days 90, 120, 150, and 180, was analyzed using analysis of covariance with fixed effects for age category, eGFR category, and baseline 24 h urinary oxalate.
L129: ## **Secondary Endpoints**
L130: The key secondary endpoint was the percentage of patients with normal or near-normal Uox.1 Other secondary endpoints included the percentage change in the summed surface area and number of kidney stones on kidney ultrasound from Baseline to Day 180, percentage change from baseline Pox on Day 180, and the rate of change in estimated glomerular filtration rate. Percentage normal or near-normal Uox was analyzed using Fisher's exact test, change from baseline oxalate was analyzed using the exact Wilcoxon two-sample test, and rate of change in eGFR was analyzed using mixed model repeated measures with a different intercept and slope for each patient. To control type 1 error, analyses were conducted in a hierarchy ordered in the following sequence:
L131: - AUC from Day 90 to Day 180, based on percentage change from Baseline in 24 h Uox,
- proportion of participants with a normalization (&lt;0.46\text{mmol}/24\text{ h}) or near-normalization (\ge 0.46 to &lt;0.60\text{mmol}/24\text{ h}) of 24 h Uox on at least two consecutive visits, beginning with Day 90,
- percentage change in Pox from Baseline to Day 180 (for adults only),
- percentage change in the number of kidney stones identified via kidney ultrasound from Baseline to Day 180,
- percentage change in the summed surface area identified via kidney ultrasound from Baseline to Day 180
- rate of change in eGFR from Baseline to Day 180.
L132: ## **Analysis Population**
L133: Despite the Agency's recommendation to conduct key efficacy analyses on the intent-to-treat (ITT) rather than the modified ITT (mITT) population, all of the Applicant's analyses were conducted on the mITT population, defined as all randomized participants who had at least one efficacy assessment after the Day 90 dosing visit. Consistent with the Agency's recommendation, analyses which conform to the ITT principle are provided in this review.
L134: 1 Normal or near normal Uox were respectively defined as 24 h Uox &lt;0.46 or \ge 0.46 to &lt;0.60\text{ mmol}/24\text{ h}, adjusted per 1.73\text{ m}^2 body surface area in participants aged &lt;18 years on at least two consecutive visits beginning with Day 90.
L135: ## **Missing Data**
L136: Missing data was imputed using the missing-at-random assumption using a fully conditional regression-based multiple imputation model, which included treatment, age category, eGFR category, baseline, and outcomes at Days 90, 120, 150, and 180. For each analysis, 50 imputations were conducted, with the results summarized using Rubin's method.
L137: ## **Sample Size Calculation**
L138: Sample size calculations were based on the primary endpoint AUC 24 h Uox from Day 90 to Day 180 for percentage change from baseline. Calculations accounted for a pre-planned, but not implemented, group sequential design, with control of alpha-spending. The randomization ratio was 2-to-1 (active-to-placebo), with a primary hypothesis test for one-sided superiority at the significance level of 2.5%. The AUC 24 h Uox in the placebo arm was assumed to be 0 and, in the active arm, was assumed to be -3600 based on a 40% decrease over the 90 days. Under these assumptions, a sample size of 36 participants (24 in the active arm and 12 in the placebo arm) would yield a power of approximately 94%.
L139: ## **Calculation of p-Values**
L140: In all of the statistical tests by the Applicant, p-values were calculated using classical statistical tests. These are not necessarily correct given the adaptive randomization conducted in the trial. Details of the adaptive randomization and the associated 'rerandomization tests' for statistical significance are provided in Section 16.2.
L141: ## **Interpretability of Results for PH2**
L142: (b) (4)

the Applicant proposed marketing approval only for patients with PH1, with results reported from a post hoc study analysis, which excluded PH2 patients.
L143: ## **Completeness Criteria and Data Collection**
L144: During data collection and analysis, to help ensure that measurement of 24 h Uox was measured over the required 24 h period, the Applicant eliminated outliers from the analysis using a 'completeness criterion.' In particular, at screening, before randomization, baseline urinary creatinine was calculated as the average concentration from two 24 h collections of urine. If the percentage change from the common average in urinary creatinine concentration was more than 20%, then the data collection for the two screening measurements was repeated a second time. After randomization, if the 24 h urine creatinine excretion changed more than 20% compared with the baseline 24 h excretion, participants were required to complete another 24 h urine collection within 10 calendar days from the time of the visit for which the collection was intended. In the event the urinary creatine in that repeated 24 h urine collection again changed more than 20% compared with the baseline 24 h excretion, the 24 h urine collection for that visit
L145: 2 The sole modification from the original analysis is a change in the analyzed population, from PH1 and PH2 to PH1 only.
L146: was not repeated. For the analyses of AUC-related endpoints, only 24 h Uox measurements that met this completeness criterion were used. If more than one measurement was collected that did not violate the completion criterion, the average was calculated for use in the analysis.
L147: Similarly, any urine collection with a duration of less than 22 h or greater than 26 h was considered invalid and was therefore repeated.
L148: ## 6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, DCR-PHXC-201
L149: ## Baseline Demographics and Disease Characteristics
L150: Baseline demographics for the ITT population showed an imbalance according to race, with all Asian participants assigned to nedosiran (Table 7, Table 8). Analyses that were conducted to understand the potential impact of this imbalance on the efficacy findings did not raise concern. In addition, the point estimate for mean baseline urinary oxalate was higher in the placebo arm; however the analysis of the primary endpoint accounted for this issue by inclusion of the Baseline value as a factor in the statistical model.

L176: ## Efficacy Results
L177: ## **Primary Efficacy Endpoint**
L178: The Applicant's analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint, 24 h Uox AUC of percentage change from Baseline calculated from Days 90 to 180, indicated efficacy, both in the prespecified analysis, which included patients with PH1 and PH2, as well as in the post hoc study analysis, which included only patients with PH1 (Table 12).

L186: Additional analyses were conducted to explore the impact of the baseline imbalance in treatment assignments among Asian participants and a reduced dosing regimen during the trial among adults weighing &lt;50 kg. Rerandomization tests (to compensate for the adaptive randomization) and tipping point analyses (to assess for the impact of missing data) were also conducted. These analyses confirmed the robustness of the primary endpoint analysis findings. See Section 16 for further discussion.
L187: ## Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
L188: ## **Urinary Oxalate**
L189: The percentage of participants with normalized or near-normalized 24 h Uox at two consecutive visits during Days 90 to 180 was statistically significantly higher in the nedosiran arm (48%) than in the placebo arm (0%) (Table 15). However, the mean Baseline value of Uox was lower in the nedosiran arm than in the placebo arm (mean baseline value of 1330\mumol/day and 1965\mumol/day, respectively). This imbalance is further illustrated in Figure 2, which shows that the lowest 10 Baseline values occurred in patients randomized to nedosiran. Given this imbalance, the results of this secondary endpoint analysis are challenging to interpret. An
L190: analysis similar to that prespecified for normalized or near-normalized urinary oxalate, did not indicate statistical significance for normalization of 24 h Uox (Table 14). Further, among the four PH1 patients randomized to nedosiran who achieved normalized urinary oxalate, two had Baseline values &lt;1000\mu\text{mol}/24\text{ h}.

L199: ## Plasma Oxalate
L200: The difference between treatments for percentage change from baseline Pox just missed the one-sided p&lt;0.025 criterion for statistical significance (Table 15). Even if the results had been statistically significant, the clinical significance of the finding would have been unclear since Pox levels are not substantially increased in patients with PH with relatively preserved kidney function, and what constitutes a clinically relevant reduction in this population is unclear. Of note, the mean treatment effect was also skewed in favor of nedosiran by data from a single subject in the placebo arm (b)(6), in whom Pox was 2 mg/L at Baseline and 9 mg/L at Day 180, resulting in a 350% increase in Pox in that patient.

L207: ## Exploratory Efficacy Endpoints
L208: Although number of kidney stones, kidney stone burden, and rate of change in eGFR were prespecified as secondary endpoints, their predecessor in the analysis hierarchy, percentage change from Baseline Pox failed to achieve statistical significance, and the results discussed below are therefore considered exploratory.
L209: ## Number of Kidney Stones and Kidney Stone Burden
L210: In Study DCR-PHX-201, the summed two-dimensional surface area of kidney stones at least 5 mm in diameter was measured using ultrasound. The nedosiran group had a median percentage decrease from Baseline in summed surface area of kidney stones of 2%, while the placebo group had a median increase of 22%; however the difference was not statistically significant (p=0.083). Per the Applicant, there was a high degree of measurement variability using two-dimensional ultrasound to quantify the surface area of kidney stones because of their “very complex and irregular” shapes. No difference was observed between the nedosiran group and the placebo group in the percent change from Baseline to Day 180 in the number of measurable kidney stones (-2.22% and -2.99%, p=0.46).
L211: Kidney stones can be asymptomatic and it is not clear whether and to what extent effects on stone burden predict clinically meaningful stone events. Further, quantifying urinary tract stones by ultrasound is prone to variability because the measurements are dependent on the equipment used and the skill of the ultrasonographer (Milliner et al. 2020).
L212: ## Rate of Change in eGFR
L213: Differences between treatment and placebo in rate of eGFR change were not statistically significant (Table 16). Although the point estimates of monthly rate of eGFR decline favor the nedosiran arm, these results should be interpreted with caution given the baseline imbalances in eGFR (median 86 mL/min/1.73 m2 [range 35 to 197 mL/min/1.73 m2] in the nedosiran group and 77 mL/min/1.73 m2 [range 44 to 131 mL/min/1.73 m2] in the placebo group) and differences in 24 h Uox (Table 8) between the treatment arms, suggesting that patients in the placebo group had more severe/advanced disease at baseline.

L220: ## Percentage Reduction in 24 h Urinary Oxalate
L221: The Applicant proposed to include information in Section 14 of the label on the percentage reduction from Baseline in Uox; however, as described in Section 16.6, the Applicant used various methods of calculation which produced conflicting results. To resolve this issue, the review team conducted an ITT analysis for percentage change in Uox directly, imputing 50 datasets for 24 h urinary oxalate measurements at Days 0, 30, 60, 90, 120, 150, and 180 and then evaluating mean change from baseline of 24 h Uox over Days 90, 120, 150, and 180 for each imputed dataset using mixed model repeated measures with independent factors treatment, eGFR group, age group, baseline 24 h Uox concentration, and visit by treatment interaction, with a compound symmetric covariance matrix, with the results subsequently combined using Rubin's rule. The effect of treatment on the percentage change from Baseline in 24 h Uox was nominally significant regardless of whether the analysis was performed for all enrolled participants or only for PH1 participants (Table 17).

L234: ## Confirmatory Evidence of Effectiveness
L235: After 6 months of treatment in PHYOX2, participants could enroll in an ongoing single-arm extension study, PHYOX3, in which all participants were treated with nedosiran. Eight patients randomized to placebo in DCR-PHXC-201 received nedosiran for 6 months in DCR-PHXC-301; 24 h urinary oxalate decreased from a mean (SD) of 1872 µmol/day (95% CI: 851, 2894) at the end of DCR-PHXC-201 to 816 µmol/day (95% CI: 591, 1042). The mean percentage decrease was 46% (95% CI: 24%, 67%), in contrast to the lack of a decrease in 24 h urinary oxalate with placebo during the double-blind portion of the trial.
L236: Additional confirmatory evidence of effectiveness is also provided from 13 participants who were enrolled in a single-dose study, DCR-PHXC-101, who also subsequently participated in Study DCR-PHXC-301. In these participants, 24 h Uox decreased from a mean (SD) baseline of 1411µmol/day (570) to 420µmol/day (272), a 69% (SD 19%) reduction after a single dose on nedosiran. Twenty-four-hour Uox then increased off treatment to a mean (SD) of 927µmol/day (279) at the time of enrollment in Study DCR-PHXC-301, and decreased to a mean of 229µmol/day (169) after restarting nedosiran in Study DCR-PHXC-301.
L237: ## Maintenance of Treatment Effect on Urinary Oxalate
L238: The reduction in Uox was maintained in 13 participants with PH1 who received 6 months of treatment with nedosiran in PHYOX2 and an additional 6 months of treatment in PHYOX3 (Table 19). Data beyond Month 6 of PHYOX3 are limited.

L243: ## Subgroup Efficacy Analyses
L244: Subgroup analyses did not indicate any differences in the treatment effect on the change in 24 h Uox between demographic subgroups, in PH1 and PH2 participants combined (Figure 3 and Table 20) and in PH1 participants alone (Figure 4 and Table 21). Subgroup analyses were not conducted according to race (only white participants in both trial arms) or ethnicity (Hispanic / Latino participants missing from placebo arm).
L245: A Bayesian hierarchical model was used to conduct the shrinkage analyses shown in these tables and figures. Specifically, for i = 1, \dots, k subgroups:
L246: - Y_i = estimated treatment effect in subgroup level i,
- \sigma_i^2 = the estimated variance for subgroup level i,
- \mu_i \sim N(\mu, \tau^2),
- \mu \sim N(0, \text{std\_dev}=\omega), \omega chosen to be 100, and
- \tau^2 \sim \text{Half-Cauchy}(0, \text{scale} = 25).

L261: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L262: ## 6.3.1. The Interpretability of AUC as Descriptor of Efficacy
L263: Interpretability and statistical properties of the Applicant's primary endpoint, AUC for percentage change from Baseline in 24 h Uox from study Days 90 to 180, and the relationship between AUC and mean percentage change in 24 h Uox.
L264: ## Background
L265: Regulations and guidance state that the principal objective for Section 14 of the label is “to provide the information that is most useful to prescribers in treating their patients,” and that the label should include “endpoints the Agency has accepted as evidence of effectiveness, or closely related endpoints that may be more easily understood.” Although the Agency did not object to the Applicant's proposal to assess the efficacy of nedosiran using the AUC for percentage change from Baseline in 24 h Uox from study Days 90 to 180, such an endpoint may not be an ideal descriptor of nedosiran's effect on Uox or easily understood by prescribers.
L266: ## Assessment
L267: Section 16.1 of the review discusses the statistical properties of the Applicant's primary endpoint and associated challenges with its interpretation as a descriptor of efficacy. Unlike mean percentage change, the value of AUC may vary according to the temporal sequence in which the underlying values of percentage change are recorded and, as a consequence, in small trials, such as the present one, AUC is more likely to generate random highs and lows in mean outcome that are not clinically meaningful. Further complicating interpretation of AUC as a measure of mean percentage change, a single value of AUC may represent several different values of mean percentage change in Uox. These two issues would be expected to add “noise” in the calculation of AUC that could bias towards the null hypothesis of no treatment effect of nedosiran. The fact that the treatment effect of nedosiran on AUC was large and highly statistically significant despite this “noise” supports the conclusion that there is a true treatment effect.
L268: One other issue with the Applicant's calculation of AUC is that it uses single imputation of missing values, in direct violation of recommendations by the National Academy of Sciences based on the fact that single imputation methods underestimate uncertainty in estimates of treatment effect. Also, the single imputation method used, linear interpolation, produces results that do not match observed data. Although these latter two issues raise uncertainty about the Uox values imputed for 87 of the 90 days used to calculate AUC, it is noteworthy that nedosiran shows consistently large reductions in Uox on Days 90, 120, 150, and 180 compared to Baseline.
L269: ## Conclusion
L270: Although the Agency did not object to the Applicant's proposal to assess the efficacy of nedosiran using this primary endpoint, it may not be easily understood by prescribers and also has undesirable statistical properties. The Applicant wishes to still include this endpoint in the labeling because it was the primary efficacy endpoint of the trial. This is acceptable but, consistent with the regulations discussed in the Background section, the review team has also
L271: requested inclusion in labeling of the least-squares mean percentage change from Baseline in 24 h urinary oxalate excretion (corrected for body surface area in patients &lt;18 years of age) averaged over Days 90, 120, 150, and 180 as this will be more easily understood by prescribers and this analysis does not have the same statistical issues as the AUC endpoint.
L272: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L273: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L274: Nonclinical studies, including safety pharmacology, general toxicology, genetic toxicology, and reproductive and developmental toxicology, were conducted to assess the safety of nedosiran in the mouse, monkey and rabbit. The key safety findings from these studies and their clinical relevance are discussed below (see detailed nonclinical study summaries in Section 13.1).
L275: ## Embryo-Fetal Development Toxicity
L276: In an embryo-fetal development study in mice, no maternal or developmental toxicities were observed with nedosiran at doses up to 2000 mg/kg/dose (58x the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) or with the mouse active analog DCR-m355.
L277: However, in rabbits, subcutaneous administration of nedosiran resulted in maternal toxicity as shown by body weight loss of 6.5% and 6%, and lower food consumption following the first dose of 6 and 20 mg/kg, respectively. Thereafter, body weights and body weight gain were comparable to the controls up to the end of the dosing period. Higher postimplantation loss and lower numbers of live fetuses were observed at doses of \ge 6 mg/kg/dose (0.7x the MRHD, based on body surface area), and fetal cardiovascular and skeletal malformations were observed at a dose of 20 mg/kg/dose (2x the MRHD). A dose level of 2 mg/kg/dose (0.2x the MRHD) had no effect on maternal toxicity and embryo-fetal development in rabbits. In the rabbit, fetal exposure was minimal, and nedosiran had no effect on RNAi-mediated changes in LDHA mRNA expression, indicating that maternal toxicity, postimplantation loss, and fetal malformations are unrelated to on-target hybridization effects, and thus, other factors could be contributing, such as extrahepatic silencing of the mRNA and/or the bulk chemical and physical properties of the molecule (e.g., high molecular weight, polyanionic macromolecules). Nedosiran did not reduce LDHA mRNA expression in the liver of the rabbit, but it reduced citrate synthase mRNA expression, which is the last step in the Krebs cycle ATP generation. Post-implantation loss may be related to the maternal toxicity in the rabbit, but the cause for fetal malformations is unclear, and could be related to citrate synthase inhibition. The relevance of the rabbit findings to humans is unknown.
L278: ## General Toxicology Studies
L279: General toxicity studies were conducted for durations up to 26 weeks in juvenile mice and 39 weeks in juvenile and adult monkeys. The ages of the animals in the nonclinical program were appropriate to support the safety evaluation for the indicated clinical population. Key findings in the pivotal toxicology studies are discussed below.
L280: ## Liver
L281: Histological changes in the liver (Kupffer cells), which included minimal to moderate macrophage basophilic hypertrophy, were observed following administration of nedosiran at a dose level of 300 mg/kg administered every 4 weeks for up to 9 months in juvenile monkey. Additional findings included minimal to mild hepatocyte basophilia at doses \ge 100 mg/kg, and minimal hypertrophy and mixed inflammatory cell infiltrates in the liver at 300 mg/kg. Electron-microscopic evaluations of the liver revealed an increase in the number and size of clear vacuoles and lysosomes within the hepatocytes, but there was no degeneration or necrosis. During the 8-week recovery period, the findings in the liver were still evident at a dose of 300 mg/kg. The hypertrophic changes in the Kupffer cell/macrophage in the liver may be due to accumulation of the test article and are considered a class effect of oligonucleotides.
L282: ## Lymph Nodes and Testes
L283: Microscopic findings of minimal to moderate macrophage basophilic hypertrophy were observed in the mesenteric lymph nodes at doses \ge 30 mg/kg, as well as in the mandibular lymph nodes and testes at a dose level of 300 mg/kg in the juvenile monkeys, indicating hybridization-independent proinflammatory effects. During the 8-week recovery period, the findings in the lymph nodes and testes were still evident at the high dose of 300 mg/kg, which could be due to accumulation of oligonucleotides in cells with macrophagic lineage. Given nedosiran's long tissue half-life, several weeks to months may be needed for full recovery.
L284: ## Injection Site Reactions
L285: At the injection sites, minimal to mild macrophage basophilic hypertrophy was observed in the subcutaneous tissues in the juvenile mice and monkeys, which resolved following an 8-week recovery period, indicating hybridization-independent effects.
L286: ## Findings in Young Adult Monkeys
L287: In the young adult cynomolgus monkey study, nedosiran was tested at one dose level of 300 mg/kg once every 4 weeks for 9 months. Key findings, including Kupffer cell basophilic hypertrophy and hepatocyte basophilia in the liver, and macrophage basophilic hypertrophy in the lymph nodes (mesenteric, mandibular), testes, and injection sites, were similar to those observed in the juvenile monkeys. There were no apparent differences in the incidence and/or severity of the findings between the juvenile and young adult monkeys.
L288: ## Conclusion for General Toxicity Studies
L289: Overall, the findings discussed above reflect cellular uptake and accumulation of oligonucleotide and were evident at high dose levels. Given the safety margin of at least 11-fold of the MRHD (see safety margins in Table 21 and Table 22 below) based on the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) determined in the monkey toxicology study, these changes are considered of minimal safety concern for the proposed clinical use.
L290: ## Safety Margins to the Maximum Recommended Human Dose
L291: The NOAEL for nedosiran in the most sensitive and relevant species, the juvenile monkey, is 100 mg/kg, with a corresponding AUC0-48h 400 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL}, which is approximately 29-fold higher
L292: than the exposure achieved in humans dosed at 2.8 mg/kg, which resulted in an AUC_{last} of 13.6 \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL} (Table 22). Nedosiran is quickly cleared from the plasma and taken up by the tissues, and the tissue residence time is very long (elimination half-life in the liver ranged from 12 to 23 days), which is much longer than the plasma distribution phase; therefore, the plasma exposure levels (AUC) may not reflect the full spectrum of the drug concentration-time curve in the tissues. For this reason, the safety margin is also estimated using body surface area-based dose scaling, which represents a more conservative (compared to AUC) estimate of 8.6-fold higher than the MRHD of 2.8 mg/kg (Table 23).

L302: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L303: ## Potential Risks With GalNAC-Conjugated siRNAs
L304: Toxicities associated with GalNAC-conjugated siRNA products include injection site reactions, serum ALT and AST elevations, increases in serum creatinine and decreases in eGFR, positive antidrug antibodies (ADA), and hypersensitivity (including anaphylactic reactions).
L305: ## Potential Risks Due to Drug-Specific Factors
L306: Nedosiran inhibits LDHA, which metabolizes glyoxylate to oxalate. In patients with PH1 treated with nedosiran, the precursor glyoxylate is expected to be converted to glycolate by glyoxylate reductase/hydroxypyruvate reductase. Effects of chronic elevations in plasma or urine glycolate are unknown; however, the available clinical and nonclinical data suggest such elevations are likely to be benign. In humans, the gene HAO1 encodes glycolate oxidase, an enzyme that converts glycolate to glyoxylate in the peroxisomes of liver cells. As such, suppression of HAO1 is expected to lead to the accumulation of glycolate. Glycolate is highly water-soluble and readily excreted in urine. In a case report published in 2014, an 8-year-old male with a homozygous splice site mutation in HAO1 was noted to have marked elevations of urinary glycolate. He had normal kidney and liver function, and no kidney stones (Frisberg et al. 2014). Similarly, glyoxalate-deficient mice (Hao1^{-/-}) have asymptomatic marked urinary glycolate
L307: elevation without phenotypic differences in urinary sediment, kidney crystal deposition, kidney histology, growth, or development compared with Hao1+/+ mice (Martin-Higueras et al. 2016).
L308: PH2 is caused by absent or decreased activity of glyoxylate reductase/hydroxypyruvate reductase; hence, glyoxylate cannot be converted to glycolate. The excess glyoxylate is converted to oxalate by LDHA. In patients with PH2 treated with nedosiran, it is not clear whether the precursor glyoxylate accumulates in cells or if it is further metabolized; hence, the upstream effects of inhibiting LDHA in patients with PH2 are not fully understood.
L309: Nedosiran is conjugated to GalNAc aminosugar residues, which bind to ASGPR to deliver nedosiran to hepatocytes; hence, nedosiran activity is expected to be targeted to the liver. Additional potential risks of nedosiran could occur if nedosiran were to inhibit extra-hepatic LDHA. In human, LDHA deficiency can occur because of recessively inherited genetic mutations in the LDHA gene, causing a deficiency of the skeletal muscle isoform of LDH, leading to glycogen storage disease XI. This condition usually presents in childhood with myopathy resulting in symptoms of fatigue, muscle pain, muscle stiffness, cramps during exercise (exercise intolerance), and rhabdomyolysis during strenuous exercise or activity.
L310: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L311: Nedosiran is not approved in the United States or any foreign market.
L312: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L313: Data from the double-blind, placebo-controlled period of Study DCR-PHXC-201 were used to support the safety of nedosiran. In addition, safety data from the ongoing single-arm Study DCR-PHXC-301, submitted on September 30, 2022 with data cutoff of January 10, 2022, were reviewed for additional safety concerns. The Safety Update Report submitted on January 27, 2023, contained additional safety data from Study DCR-PHXC-301 (data cutoff of August 11, 2022) and was reviewed for any new findings.
L314: Safety data from Studies DCR-PHXC-201 and -301 were not pooled given differences in study design. Safety analyses used the Safety Population, defined as all patients who received any amount of study drug.
L315: The safety review focused on known and potential toxicities of nedosiran based on its mechanism of action, nonclinical findings, and the experience with other GalNAc-conjugated siRNA products. These known and potential toxicities included injection site reactions, hepatic and kidney toxicity, immunogenicity, and hypersensitivity reactions.
L316: Adverse events (AEs) were analyzed by Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) v. 23.1 preferred term and by pooling similar AEs (referred to as the FDA Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities Query [FMQ]). The FMQ analysis is similar to a customized Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities query. Adverse events of interest specified by the Applicant included injection site reactions (defined as signs or symptoms at the injection site with a time to onset of four or more hours from the time of study drug administration), kidney stone events, and muscle pain or weakness. A treatment-emergent
L317: adverse event (TEAE) was defined as an AE that started, changed in intensity, or changed relationship to the study treatment at the time of or after the first dose date and before the study completion date.
L318: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L319: The clinical safety database is limited given the size of the affected population. In the placebo-controlled Study, DCR-PHXC-201, 23 patients \ge9 years of age received nedosiran for a mean duration of 4.7 months (Table 24).

L325: As previously noted, the Applicant has also submitted uncontrolled safety data from their ongoing single-arm Study (DCR-PHXC-301), which enrolled participants with PH who (1) had previously completed a study of nedosiran, or (2) are pediatric siblings with PH of participants who successfully completed a study of DCR-PHXC and who met eligibility criteria. Following completion of DCR-PHXC-201, 22 of 23 participants randomized to nedosiran and 11 of 12 participants randomized to placebo enrolled in Study DCR-PHXC-301 and received open-label nedosiran. As of data cutoff of August 11, 2022, Study DCR-PHXC-301 had enrolled a total of 54 participants with PH and relatively intact kidney function, including 40 participants with PH1, 10 with PH2, and four with PH3. All but one participant (a sibling 12 years of age with PH2) had rolled over from a previous study of nedosiran. As of data cutoff, the mean (SD) and median (min, max) durations of exposure were 21 (9) months and 21 (0.9, 36) months, respectively. For participants that were enrolled in Study DCR-PHXC-201, this duration of exposure reflects the sum of the exposure in Studies DCR-PHXC-201 and -301; for participants that rolled over from single dose studies (DCR-PHXC-101 and DCR-PHXC-104), the duration of exposure does not include the single-dose period. Thirty-five participants (64%) have been on nedosiran for 18 months or longer.
L326: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L327: ## 7.6.1. Safety Results, Pooled Analyses, DCR-PHXC-201 and DCR-PHXC-301
L328: The review team did not conduct integrated analyses of safety because of differences in the design of Studies DCR-PHXC-201 and DCR-PHXC-301. See Sections 7.6.2 and 7.6.3 for analyses of individual trial results.
L329: ## 7.6.2. Safety Results, DCR-PHXC-201
L330: ## 7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, DCR-PHXC-201
L331: The proportion of participants with TEAEs was similar between the treatment groups (Table 25). Most TEAEs were mild or moderate in severity. There were two serious adverse events (SAEs) in the placebo arm compared with one SAE with nedosiran. There were no deaths. One participant in each treatment arm discontinued study drug because of an AE (see Section 7.6.2.4 for details).

L351: The participant (b) (6) who experienced an SAE (tachycardia) in the nedosiran arm discontinued treatment and withdrew consent because of the SAE. The narrative for this patient is provided below; based on the narrative, it seems unlikely that the SAE was related to nedosiran.
L352: - *Participant (b) (6): A 14-year-old male with a history of PH1, hematuria, myopia, malnutrition, and iron deficiency. His baseline weight was 43.7 kg. The patient was on concomitant potassium citrate, mouthwash for gingivitis (aspegic [a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory agent], lidocaine, and sodium bicarbonate), paracetamol, phloroglucinol (used for spasmodic gastrointestinal pain), and Fortimel (a nutritional high protein supplement). The patient had no reported previous cardiac history.*

*- Baseline heart rate was 83 beats per minute (bpm), blood pressure 105/71 mm Hg, hemoglobin 13.4 g/dL, blood urea nitrogen 17 mg/dL, serum creatinine 1.06 mg/dL, and serum albumin 4.4 g/dL. The patient was randomized to nedosiran and received the first dose of 136 mg by subcutaneous injection on Day 1 (b) (6) and the second dose on Day 28 (b) (6). On Day 34 (b) (6), 6 days after the second dose of nedosiran, the patient complained of palpitations, tachycardia, and chest pain. On Day 35 (b) (6) the patient was seen in the emergency department for these symptoms. Blood pressure was 108/61 mmHg, chest X-ray was reportedly normal, electrocardiogram (ECG) revealed a heart rate of 72 bpm, hemoglobin was 13.4 g/dL, serum troponin was normal, serum electrolytes were unremarkable, blood*
L353: urea nitrogen was 24 mg/dL, and serum creatinine was 1.15 mg/dL. He was discharged home on paracetamol, but returned the next day (b) (6); Day 36) and was admitted to the hospital for persistent chest pain, tachycardia, and palpitations. His heart rate in the hospital, captured on telemetry, was noted to intermittently increase from approximately 70 bpm to 140 to 150 bpm for about 1 to 3 min. Holter ECG from (b) (6), was read as “sinus accelerations” without specific arrhythmia (e.g., supraventricular tachycardia, atrial tachycardia, or ventricular extrasystoles). The chest pain and tachycardia were resolved by Day 39 (b) (6). On Day 49 (b) (6) transthoracic echocardiogram was normal.
L354: - Based on a recommendation from the Data Safety and Monitoring Committee, nedosiran was discontinued (no additional doses were administered after the second dose). The participant withdrew from the study on Day 111 (b) (6). No additional AEs were reported for this participant after Day 39.
- The clinical data and Holter ECG were reviewed by independent pediatric and adult electrophysiology cardiologists. The pediatric cardiologist noted that the Holter ECG was normal for the participant's age and gender, the tachycardia was likely sinus although an atrial nodal re-entrant tachycardia could not be ruled out. The adult cardiologist interpreted the Holter ECG as falling into the “bin” of supraventricular tachycardias, most likely an ectopic atrial tachycardia, or less likely atrioventricular-nodal or atrioventricular-accessory-pathway reentrant tachycardia. Both cardiologists did not believe the tachycardia was likely caused by nedosiran.
- Although there appears to be a temporal relationship between the participant's second dose of nedosiran and intermittent self-resolving tachycardia, other factors could have contributed to the tachycardia in this patient, e.g., anxiety (according to the Clinical Study Report, the patient reported anxiety) or possibly a panic attack. The participant had also begun exercising, although it is not clear that the patient was exercising during the episodes of tachycardia. It is also not clear that the patient had an arrhythmia; there appeared to be disagreement between the two cardiology experts. As noted, the pediatric cardiologist's assessment was that the tachycardia was likely sinus. Even if the participant did have supraventricular tachycardia, this is the most common rhythm disturbance in children, commonly due to an atrial nodal re-entrant tachycardia in this age group, and unlikely related to nedosiran. Further, because study drug was discontinued, the participant was not rechallenged with nedosiran to see if the tachycardia would recur. No other participant in the safety database has had an AE of tachycardia or arrhythmia.
L355: One participant in the placebo group (b) (6) with PH1 discontinued study drug on Day 112 because of an SAE of blood creatinine increased, progression of chronic kidney disease (CKD) and initiation of dialysis.

L364: ## 7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, DCR-PHXC-201
L365: **Table 28.** shows TEAEs by System Organ Class and narrow FMQs that occurred in two or more participants and with a risk difference of &gt;5% in the nedosiran group compared with placebo. The most common TEAEs were local administration reactions (injection site reactions) followed by hemorrhage, nausea, and bacterial infection. The size of the safety database is limited and some events, e.g., bacterial infections, were infrequent, hence, limiting conclusions regarding their relationship to nedosiran.
L366: TEAEs that mapped to the narrow FMQ Hemorrhage were reported in three participants in the nedosiran group and none in placebo. These TEAEs included hematuria, injection site bruising, and metrorrhagia. Except for the TEAE of injection site bruising, the other events of hematuria (common in patients with kidney stones) and metrorrhagia were likely not related to nedosiran. Similarly, TEAEs that mapped to the narrow FMQ of Bacterial Infection included urinary tract infections, which are frequently associated with kidney stones in patients with PH, and tooth abscess, were likely not related to nedosiran. There is no obvious mechanistic basis by which nedosiran could cause hematuria, metrorrhagia, urinary tract infection, or tooth abscess; therefore, those events will not be discussed further.
L367: Review of all TEAEs by Preferred Term (**Table 90**) identified one additional TEAE of neutrophil count decreased in two participants (9%) in the nedosiran group and none in placebo, for a risk difference of 9% (95% CI -3, 20). Participants (b) (6) and (b) (6) had a decrease in neutrophil count to 1.5 and 0.5x109/L on a single assessment on Day 15 and Day 90, respectively. In both participants, the neutrophil counts returned to the normal range at the next assessment despite the participants remaining on nedosiran, suggesting that nedosiran did not play a causative role.

L374: ## **Local Administration Reactions (Injection Site Reactions)**
L375: Local administration reactions occurred more frequently in the nedosiran group (seven participants, 30%) compared with placebo (zero participants). Participants could receive up to six injections during the 6-month placebo-controlled period. Three of the seven participants (43%) experienced a reaction with one dose, and two participants (29%) had a reaction with all six doses. Local administration reactions included erythema, pain, bruising, and rash. The reactions were generally mild and did not lead to treatment discontinuation. A total of 23 reactions were reported, of which 5 (22%) resolved within 1 day of the injection. The median time for resolution was 12 days after the injection (range 0 to 99 days).
L376: ## Nausea
L377: Four participants (17%) in the nedosiran group and one (8%) in the placebo group reported AEs of nausea. Of the participants in the nedosiran group with nausea, three participants reported nausea within two days after a nedosiran dose, and one reported nausea 10 days after the nedosiran dose. The median duration of nausea was 8 days (range 1 to 152 days). All episodes were mild, did not lead to study drug interruption or discontinuation, and all resolved (it is not clear if additional treatments were administered for the nausea). Additional details are provided below for the participants randomized to nedosiran who developed nausea. There is no obvious mechanistic basis for such an effect and based on the provided information, including the time course for the events relative to nedosiran administration, it seems unlikely that nedosiran played a role.
L378: - *Participant* [REDACTED](b) (6): A 10-year-old female with a history of PH2, gastrostomy, attention deficit hyperactivity disorder, and seizure. The participant was on concomitant potassium and sodium phosphate, ethosuximide, and diazepam. The participant had an AE of nausea on Day 58, the same day as the third nedosiran dose. The participant continued nedosiran dosing, the last dose in DCR-PHXC-201 was administered on Day 149 [REDACTED](b) (6). The nausea was reported to have resolved on Day 209 [REDACTED](b) (6) i.e., a duration of 152 days. The participant continued to receive nedosiran in the extension Study (DCR-PHXC-301).
- *Participant* [REDACTED](b) (6): A 46-year-old female with a history of PH1, kidney stones, angiomylipoma, cystitis, drug hypersensitivity, hypertension, mumps, kidney cyst, rubella, ureteral stent insertion, and varicella. The participant was on concomitant amiloride/hydrochlorothiazide, magnesium, metamizole, paracetamol, potassium citrate, telmisartan, and pyridoxine hydrochloride. The participant experienced an AE of nausea on Day 3 [REDACTED](b) (6), 2 days after the first dose of nedosiran (on [REDACTED](b) (6)). The episode was reported as resolved after 2 days, on Day 4 [REDACTED](b) (6). The participant remained on nedosiran and completed the study.
- *Participant* [REDACTED](b) (6): A 17-year-old female with a history of PH2, and lithotripsy. The participant was on concomitant potassium citrate and paracetamol as needed. On Day 96 [REDACTED](b) (6) 10 days after her fourth nedosiran dose, the participant reported an AE of nausea, which resolved on the same day. The participant remained on nedosiran and completed the study.
- *Participant* [REDACTED](b) (6): A 36-year-old female with a history of PH1, asthma, irritable bowel syndrome, migraine, muscle spasms, ureteral stent insertion, and urethral cyst. The participant was on concomitant magnesium, krill oil, betamethasone dipropionate, budesonide, formoterol fumarate, codeine, drosiprenone, and ethinylestradiol betadex. The participant experienced an AE of nausea on Day 3 [REDACTED](b) (6), 2 days after the first dose of nedosiran (on [REDACTED](b) (6)). The episode lasted for 13 days and was reported as resolved on Day 15 [REDACTED](b) (6). The participant remained on nedosiran and completed the study.

It is not known when the event resolved; the final outcome of the AE was reported as unknown. The participant experienced intermittent increases of creatine kinase (CK) levels (see additional details below). CK was normal when
L391: assessed on Days 45, 60, and 90. Nedosiran dosing was not interrupted or discontinued and the patient continued to receive nedosiran in the extension study (DCR-PHXC-301).
